) towards equilibrium takes place on the larger one, which can still be smaller than the body size.

A relation between the perfect fluid four-velocity and Killing vectors, for stationary axially symmetric rotating stars,
42
42

42

Note that
Gourgoulhon 2010
works with the enthalpy per particle instead of chemical potentials. However, this makes no difference for barotropic perfect fluids.
has been discussed by
Gourgoulhon 2010
. A similar discussion about thermodynamic equilibrium in General Relativity and the existence of Killing vectors was provided by
Becattini 2016
. Specifically, he showed that there must be global Killing vector fields if the total entropy of the system is to be independent of the spacelike hypersurface over which the integration is performed. As for the work presented here, we will now show under what conditions the combination
\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}=\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{-1}u^{a}_{\rm e}
can be turned into Killing vector fields.

From
eq.

129
it can be seen that

\begin{split}\nabla_{a}\xi_{b}^{\mathrm{x}}+\nabla_{b}\xi_{a}^{\mathrm{x}}=\frac{1}{\mu_{\mathrm{x}}}\big(u^{\rm e}_{(a}u^{\rm e}_{b)}\dot{\text{log}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}}+D_{(a}u^{\rm e}_{b)}\big)\;,\end{split}

(132)

so that, if dynamical suppression has worked, the right hand side becomes zero and the
\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
will be a timelike Killing vector field, along which the local thermodynamical parameters
n_{\mathrm{x}}
,
\mu^{\mathrm{x}}
,
{\varepsilon}
, and
p
become constants of motion. Put in different words, if we want the system to be (at least quasi-locally) at equilibrium—i.e. stationary— we also need to require rigid body motion.

9.5
A final comment on equilibrium

To conclude we will come full circle and consider again the change in total entropy given by
eq.

108
. The result only references spacelike hypersurfaces as part of the (ad hoc) choice of the boundary of the spacetime region for which the entropy change is being determined. There are no restrictions placed on the spacetime geometry in this construct; in particular, no requirement of global Killing vectors.
As a matter of practice, the change in entropy of a system is clearly dependent on the spatial size and the amount of time the system has had to evolve. Coupling this with the fact that a separation of space from time is always a choice—an arbitrary spacetime has no preferred directions, no natural “moments-of-time”—we see that the ad hoc nature of the boundary in
eq.

108
is not a drawback. This is precisely the freedom needed in order to incorporate a system’s spatial extent and evolution time, and the fact that a separation of space from time in spacetime is always a choice.

The main reason why this is intriguing is that the second law of thermodynamics only refers to the change in total entropy, not the value of entropy itself at specific moments of time (i.e. spacelike hypersurfaces). It may be that questions of equilibrium are not to be settled by the “moment-to-moment” behaviour of three-dimensional integrals, but rather by global statements of the type represented by
eq.

108
. This is something we are currently investigating and hope to be able to give more detail on in a future work.

10
Perturbations with respect to equilibrium

With the equations of motion obtained from an action principle, we can consider perturbations away from equilibrium configurations (of the kind described above) in a way that is closely related—at least from the formal perspective—to standard hydrodynamical perturbation theory. The general approach to Lagrangian perturbation theory is perhaps best described by
Friedman and Schutz 1975
. Roughly speaking, the evolution equations for the perturbed fields can be obtained by perturbing the equations that follow from the action. It is also clear—at least in principle—how to construct a Lagrangian whose variation gives the perturbed equations (see §2 of
[
82
]
). However, since we are not focussing on a stability analysis of fluid oscillations we will not consider this additional aspect here.

To set the stage for the perturbative expansion, we consider the family of worldlines (not necessarily geodesics) that each constituent of a multifluid system traces out in spacetime. Our definition of equilibrium includes the assumption that all species are comoving. Therefore, our fiducial set of worldlines representing equilibrium are those the system would have followed if it were comoving throughout its history.

Figure 3
:
An illustration of worldlines associated with the fluid elements (solid vertical red lines, parameterized by
\tau,\,\bar{\tau}
) and “Lagrangian displacements” which connect fluid elements (dashed horizontal blue lines, parameterized by
\lambda
).

This then allows us to view each of the “final” worldlines
x_{\rm f}^{a}(\bar{\tau})
as a curve in spacetime which is close to the equilibrium one
x_{\rm e}^{a}(\tau)
, with
\bar{\tau}
and
\tau
being the proper times of the respective curves. See
fig.

3
for an illustration of the idea. The unit four-velocities associated with the two worldlines are

u^{a}_{\rm f}=\frac{dx_{\rm f}^{a}}{d\bar{\tau}}\;,\qquad u^{a}_{\rm e}=\frac{dx_{\rm e}^{a}}{d\tau}\;.

(133)

Obviously,
u^{a}_{\rm e}
represents the comoving frame introduced earlier.

We assume another family of curves
x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}(\lambda)
, where
\lambda
is an affine parameter (say, the proper length), that connects the equilibrium worldline to the actual one. This means that for any point
x_{\rm e}^{a}(\tau_{e})
on the equilibrium worldline, there is a unique point
x_{\rm f}^{a}(\bar{\tau}_{f})
on the perturbed worldline, and a unique curve
x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}(\lambda)
between them having two points
x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}\left(\lambda_{\rm e}\right)
and
x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}\left(\lambda_{\rm f}\right)
such that

x_{\rm f}^{a}\left(\bar{\tau}_{\rm f}\right)=x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}\left(\lambda_{\rm f}\right)\;,\qquad x_{\rm e}^{a}\left(\tau_{\rm e}\right)=x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}\left(\lambda_{\rm e}\right)\;.

(134)

Taylor expanding the perturbed worldline about the equilibrium up to second order, we get

\begin{split}x_{\rm f}^{a}(\bar{\tau}_{\rm f})&=x_{\rm e}^{a}(\tau_{\rm e})+\frac{dx_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}}{d\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda_{\rm e}}(\lambda_{\rm f}-\lambda_{\rm e})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{2}x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}}{d^{2}\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda_{\rm e}}(\lambda_{\rm f}-\lambda_{\rm e})^{2}\\
&=x_{\rm e}^{a}(\tau_{\rm e})+\zeta^{a}\Delta\lambda+\frac{1}{2}\Big(\zeta^{b}\partial_{b}\zeta^{a}\Big)\Delta\lambda^{2}\;,\end{split}

(135)

where we introduced the tangent vector

\frac{d}{d\lambda}=\frac{dx_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}}{d\lambda}\Big|_{\lambda_{\rm e}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{a}}=\zeta^{a}\partial_{a}\;.

(136)

The first objects we want to perturb are the fluid element “names”. That is, we attach a label
X^{A}
, where the index
A=1,2,3
, to each of the worldlines used to cover the region of spacetime occupied by the fluid. By definition of the Lagrangian variation
[
82
,
83
]
we then have

\Delta X^{A}=\Big(\phi_{*}X^{A}(x_{\rm f})\Big)(x_{\rm e})-\bar{X}^{A}(x_{\rm e})=X^{A}(x_{\rm f})-\bar{X}^{A}(x_{\rm e})=0\;,

(137)

where
\phi
is the diffeomorphism that connects the perturbed and unperturbed worldlines, via the flow lines
x_{{\rm e}{\rm f}}^{a}
, and and
\phi_{*}
denotes the pull-back from perturbed to equilibrium manifold.
The last equality then follows from the fact that the fluid label does not change as we follow it. As a result we have, to first order

\delta X^{A}=-\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\mathrm{x}}}X^{A}=-\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}=-\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}\;,

(138)

where we introduced the Lagrangian displacement vector
\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}=x_{\rm f}^{a}-x_{\rm e}^{a}
.

It is important to note that these displacement vectors are different from the ones introduced when obtaining the equations of motion from the action principle (see
eq.

68
), even though the mathematics appears the same. In the present case the displacement vector connects two configurations that are “close” in the space of physical solutions—in field-theory parlance they are both “on-shell”. We also note that, to compute the second order variation we cannot rely on the simple relation that exists between Lagrangian and Eulerian variation (at first order). We need to perform such calculations explicitly.

At this point, it is worth pausing to consider what is behind the perturbation scheme we are building. Since we assume the existence of a well defined equilibrium timelike congruence
x_{\rm e}^{a}
with four velocity
u_{\rm e}^{a}
, we may imagine riding along with the equilibrium fluid element observing the evolution of the system (towards equilibrium) from this perspective. This means that the x-species four-velocity
u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}
can be decomposed (in the usual way) as

u^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}=\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}\Big(u_{\rm e}^{a}+w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\Big)\;,\text{ where }\quad w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u_{a}^{\rm e}=0\;,\quad\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}=\Big(1-w^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{a}^{\mathrm{x}}\Big)^{-1/2}\;.

(139)

Moreover, since we are working up to first order we have

\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}=1+\frac{1}{2}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}\approx 1+{\mathcal{O}}(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=u_{\rm e}^{a}+w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\;.

(140)

We note that this linear expansion in the relative velocities, although in a different spirit, has also been discussed in the context of extensions to magneto-hydrodynamics
[
16
,
24
,
25
]
.

Also, it is interesting in itself (and necessary for perturbing the full set of fluid equations) to understand the relation between the spatial velocity
w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}
as measured by the equilibrium observer and the Lagrangian displacement
\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
. We consider the displacement to live in the local present of the equilibrium observer, i.e., to be such that
\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u^{\rm e}_{a}=\zeta_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u^{\rm e}_{a}=0
.
43
43

43

This is essentially a gauge choice, see
[
21
]
for discussion.
This implies that the vectors
\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
and
\zeta^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
are spacelike non-null vector fields in spacetime. As a result, if we consider the proper time of the perturbed worldline, we have

-d\bar{\tau}^{2}=g_{ab}\,dx^{a}_{\rm f}\,dx^{b}_{\rm f}=g_{ab}\,dx_{\rm e}^{a}\,dx_{\rm e}^{b}+g_{ab}\Big(dx_{\rm e}^{a}\,\zeta^{b}\Delta\lambda+dx_{\rm e}^{b}\,\zeta^{a}\Delta\lambda\Big)=-d\tau^{2}\;,

(141)

where we used the fact that

x_{\rm e}^{a}=x_{\rm e}^{a}(\tau)\Longrightarrow dx_{\rm e}^{a}=u_{\rm e}^{a}d\tau\;.

(142)

As a consequence, the proper time of the perturbed and equilibrium worldline is the same, so we have

u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\frac{dx_{\rm f}^{a}}{d\bar{\tau}}\approx\frac{dx_{\rm e}^{a}}{d\tau}+\frac{d}{d\tau}\xi^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}=u_{\rm e}^{a}+\dot{\xi}_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\;,

(143)

where (again) the dot represents the covariant directional derivative in the direction of the equilibrium four-velocity.
44
44

44

To be more precise, one should distinguish between
\frac{d}{d\tau}=u^{b}_{\rm e}\partial_{b}
and
\frac{D}{D\tau}=u^{b}_{\rm e}\nabla_{b}
. Since we are introducing a decomposition of a vector as a sum of two,
\dot{\xi}^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
must be a vector as well so that the dot represents a covariant directional derivative.
We observe that from the construction we have
w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\dot{\xi}^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
and it is clear that when pushing the expansion to second order their relation will become more involved—both because the difference between the proper times (
\bar{\tau}
versus
\tau
) appears at second order and because the Taylor expansion gets more complicated.

We now aim to understand how to construct the expansion directly in matter space. We start by noting that, since we are considering each displacement
\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}
to be orthogonal to
u^{a}_{\rm e}
there is no loss of information in projecting the Lagrangian displacements onto the equilibrium matter space and dealing with
\xi^{A}_{\mathrm{x}}
. The general picture is thus as follows: in the general non-linear theory each matter space can be considered as an independent but interacting manifold, but this changes when we consider a perturbative expansion. In fact, the fundamental assumption of perturbation theory is that the two configurations (perturbed and unperturbed) are related by some diffeomorphism. This implies that the perturbed and unperturbed matter spaces
45
45

45

Recall that the matter space is obtained by taking the quotient of the spacetime over the corresponding worldline, i.e. identifying the worldline as a single point.
are diffeomorphic, that is they are
the same
abstract manifold. Therefore we can use the same chart on the two manifolds
X^{A}
(label the worldlines in the same way) and the difference will be only in that
X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}(x^{a})\neq X_{\rm e}^{A}(x^{a})
. The difference between the two will be exactly what we found above, namely
-\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}
. We also note that, by our definition of the unperturbed state, all the perturbed matter spaces are diffeomorphic to the same unperturbed one, and thus to each other.

Given this, we can work out how a general matter space tensor transforms under diffeomorphisms
[
50
]
. For instance, if we consider the projected metric
g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}
we have
46
46

46

For the Lie derivative we use the formula with partial derivatives in order to avoid the possible confusion arising from the choice of the connection used on the matter space.

\delta g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}=-\mathcal{L}_{-\xi_{\mathrm{x}}}g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}=\mathcal{L}_{\xi_{\mathrm{x}}}g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}=\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{C}\partial_{C}g_{\rm e}^{AB}-g_{\rm e}^{CB}\partial_{C}\xi^{A}_{\mathrm{x}}-g_{\rm e}^{AC}\partial_{C}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{B}\;,

(144)

where the partial derivatives are taken with respect to the equilibrium matter space coordinates. We now observe that, considering
\xi^{A}_{\mathrm{x}}
as a scalar field in spacetime we can write

-g_{\rm e}^{CB}\partial_{C}\xi^{A}=-g^{ab}\Psi^{C}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}\partial_{C}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}=-\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}\nabla^{b}\xi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}}\;.

(145)

We also note that, since
47
47

47

If this is not immediately convincing one can prove it by taking the explicit definition of a derivative on the coordinate functions
X^{A}(\bar{X})=\delta^{A}_{\,C}\bar{X}^{C}=\bar{X}^{A}
and using the linearity of the derivative.

\partial_{C}\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}=\partial_{a}\delta^{A}_{C}=0,
we have

\partial_{C}g_{\rm e}^{AB}=2\,g^{ab}\Bigg(\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{\rm e}^{C}}\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Bigg)\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}=0\;.

(146)

As a result, the projected metrics transform as

\delta g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}=-\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,a}\nabla^{a}\xi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}}-\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\nabla^{a}\xi^{B}_{{\mathrm{x}}}\;.

(147)

This also tells us that building the variation of the metric tensor in this way, we are only comparing the difference in the position of the particles, keeping fixed the spacetime metric.

We can now use the definition in
eq.

118
to decompose the displacement gradients as

\nabla_{a}\xi_{{\mathrm{x}}}^{A}=-w_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}u^{\rm e}_{a}+D_{a}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}\;,

(148)

and rewrite

\begin{split}\delta g^{AB}_{\mathrm{x}}&=\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,a}(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}u^{a}_{\rm e}-D^{a}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A})+\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{B}u^{a}_{\rm e}-D^{a}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{B})\\
&=-D^{B}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}-D^{A}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{B}\;,\end{split}

(149)

where we introduced the short-hand notation
D^{A}=\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,b}\,g^{ab}D_{a}
. It is worth noting that
eq.

149
is not a strain-rate tensor of the type usually introduced in fluid dynamics, because it involves gradients in the displacements instead of velocities. The usual strain rate tensor is in fact
48
48

48

To see this one has to use
\mathcal{L}_{u_{\mathrm{x}}}\Psi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,a}=0
.

\begin{split}\dot{g}_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}&=-2\,\Psi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,(a}\Psi^{B}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,b)}\big[-u_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}\dot{u}^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}+\varpi^{ab}_{\mathrm{x}}+\sigma^{ab}_{\mathrm{x}}+\frac{1}{3}\theta_{\mathrm{x}}\perp^{ab}_{\mathrm{x}}\big]=\\
&=-2\,\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,(a}\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b)}\big(\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}^{ab}+\frac{1}{3}\theta_{\mathrm{x}}\perp^{ab}_{\rm e}\big)+\mathcal{O}(2)=-2\big(\sigma_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}+\frac{1}{3}\theta_{\mathrm{x}}g^{AB}_{\rm e}\big)\;,\end{split}

(150)

We will comment on the implications of this difference later.

Even if it is not entirely obvious what kind of object the mixed projected metric
g^{AB}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}
is in the general non-linear case, in the context of a perturbative expansion there is no real difference between the various matter spaces (they are all diffeomorphic to the equilibrium one). This means that we can use the same fundamental formula also for
g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}
to get

\begin{split}\delta g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}&=g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}-g_{\rm e}^{AB}=g^{ab}\Big(\delta\Psi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,a}\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}+\delta\Psi^{B}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,b}\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Big)=\\
&=-\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,a}\nabla^{a}\xi^{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}}-\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\nabla^{a}\xi^{B}_{{\mathrm{y}}}\;.\end{split}

(151)

It is interesting to note that since
\delta g^{ab}=0
we
have

[\delta,\nabla_{a}]=[\delta,\partial_{a}]=0\;.

(152)

That is, the variation commutes with both partial and covariant derivatives. This will become relevant when we need to work out the variation of the resistive terms that stem from a dependence of the
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
on
g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}
and
g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}
.

As there has been a number of recent efforts aimed at building first-order dissipative hydrodynamic models starting from a field-theory perspective (cf.
section

7
), it makes sense to point out the differences between the present expansion and the field-theory-based ones.
In that context, the models are said to be of first order if the constitutive equations involve all permissible terms with just one derivative—as when the system is close to equilibrium one can expect the gradients in temperature, chemical potential etc
\dots
to be small, so that terms with two or more derivatives are dominated by first-order ones.
In contrast, we here assume the variables that define the physical state of the system to take values close to the equilibrium ones, and by “first order” we mean the deviations are expanded up to
\mathcal{O}(\xi_{\mathrm{x}})
. It is therefore clear that the present approach differs from the field-theory-based (gradient) expansions. The ultimate reason is that the action-based model provides the exact equations, which we then approximate, while in the field-theory approach one is trying to build the full equations as successive expansions.

11
Energy density is stationary at equilibrium

As discussed in
section

4.2
, in order to describe out-of-equilibrium systems with the Extended Irreversible Thermodynamic (EIT) paradigm, one postulates the existence of a generalized entropy—a function of a larger set of Degrees of Freedom than the corresponding equilibrium ones—which is maximized at equilibrium. The starting point for the formalism used here is a generalized energy where the only degrees of freedom are the fluxes. The action-based model provides the total stress-energy-momentum tensor
T_{ab}
of the system, so that we can easily extract the total energy density
{\varepsilon}
for some observer having four-velocity
u^{a}
via the projection
{\varepsilon}=u^{a}u^{b}T_{ab}
. We will now show that requiring the local energy density to be at a minimum in equilibrium means the viscous stress tensors have to be zero.

When specific modeling is carried out, such as a numerical evolution, we would need to provide an equation of state and specify values for the microphysical input parameters. From the phenomenological point of view, this corresponds to assuming the existence of a function—in our case, energy density—defined on some “thermodynamical manifold” whose coordinates are the relevant degrees of freedom. Practically speaking, the formalism developed here suggests we may identify the thermodynamical manifold with the matter space used in the variational model. As the general discussion gets quite complex, we focus on the specific example of a two-component system, with the components representing matter and entropy (see
[
142
,
22
]
).

Let us first consider the non-dissipative limit. Thermodynamics of a single fluid is described by some equilibrium energy
\varepsilon_{\rm e}(n,s)
such that

d\varepsilon_{\rm e}=Tds+\mu dn=\sum_{{\mathrm{x}}={\rm n},{\rm s}}\mu^{\mathrm{x}}dn_{\mathrm{x}}\;.

(153)

On the other hand, the conservative variational model is built using a master function
\Lambda(n_{\rm n}^{2},n_{\rm s}^{2},n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2})
. Because of our assumption that all species are comoving in equilibrium there is no heat flux relative to the matter and therefore
n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}=-g_{ab}n_{\rm n}^{a}n_{\rm s}^{b}=+n_{\rm n}n_{\rm s}
, and the master function only depends on two variables,
\Lambda_{\rm e}=\Lambda_{\rm e}(n_{\rm n},n_{\rm s})
. It is indeed easy to see that the equilibrium energy density, as measured by the equilibrium observer, is

\varepsilon_{\rm e}=T_{ab}^{\rm e}\,u_{\rm e}^{a}u_{\rm e}^{b}=\big[\Psi_{\rm e}g_{ab}+(\Psi_{\rm e}-\Lambda_{\rm e})u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\big]u_{\rm e}^{a}u_{\rm e}^{b}=-\Lambda_{\rm e}\;.

(154)

Since we have already identified the matter space normalizations of the three-forms with the rest frame densities
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}=n_{\mathrm{x}}
, we can think of the thermodynamic energy as a function defined on the matter space, and write

\varepsilon_{\rm e}=\varepsilon_{\rm e}({\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n},{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s})=-\Lambda_{\rm e}({\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n},{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s})\;.

(155)

The equilibrium case suggests that we could try to extend this identification to the non-equilibrium setting, and “build” the thermodynamics on the matter space. This raises the (difficult) question of what the global matter space is in the full non-linear case. We will not address that issue here. Instead, we focus on the near-equilibrium case, where we only have to deal with the equilibrium matter space.

Because of the way we have built the expansion, it is natural to project tensor quantities —fluxes, stress-energy-momentum tensor, etcetera—into the frame of the equilibrium observer, as defined by the equilibrium worldlines congruence
u^{a}_{\rm e}
. Quantities measured in this frame will be indicated by a “hat” in the following. Objects without a hat are measured in fluid rest frames, which are defined by the
u^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
. The equilibrium value of a quantity in the equilibrium frame will be indicated with a “bar”. For instance, the particle density measured in the equilibrium frame is
\hat{n}_{\mathrm{x}}=-u^{\rm e}_{a}n^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
; in the
{\mathrm{x}}
-fluid rest frame it is
n_{\mathrm{x}}=-u^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}n^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
; and the equilibrium value in the equilibrium frame is
\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}=\hat{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\big|_{\rm e}
.

The “out-of-equilibrium” energy density
\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}
of the system as determined in the equilibrium rest frame is given by

\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}=\big(T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}D^{ab}_{\mathrm{x}}\big)u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}=\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}^{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}+D^{ab}u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\;,

(156)

where we have separated the contribution from the viscous stress tensor
D_{ab}
from those having the “non-dissipative” form

T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}=\Big(\Lambda-\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}^{c}\mu^{\mathrm{x}}_{c}\Big)g^{ab}+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}=\Psi\,g^{ab}+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}\;.

(157)

The expression for
\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}
can be made more explicit by means of
eq.

127
, which leads to
\Psi=\Lambda+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}
and

\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}^{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}=u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}=-\Lambda-\sum_{{\mathrm{x}}=n,s}\big(n_{\mathrm{x}}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}-\hat{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}\big)\;.

(158)

Because the flux is a vector, the two densities
\hat{n}_{\mathrm{x}}
and
n_{\mathrm{x}}
are easily shown to be related by

\hat{n}_{\mathrm{x}}=-n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u_{a}=-n_{\mathrm{x}}u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u_{a}=(1-w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}w^{\mathrm{x}}_{a})^{-1/2}n_{\mathrm{x}}=\Big(1+\frac{1}{2}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}\Big)n_{\mathrm{x}}+\mathcal{O}(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{3})\;.

(159)

Meanwhile, the corresponding momentum relation is a bit more involved because of entrainment:

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}&=-\mu^{\mathrm{x}}_{b}u_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}=-\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}(u^{b}+w_{\mathrm{x}}^{b})\big(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}u^{\mathrm{x}}_{b}+\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}n_{\mathrm{y}}u_{\mathrm{y}}^{b}\big)\\
&=\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}\Big(\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}-\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}\gamma_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}-\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}n_{\mathrm{y}}\gamma_{\mathrm{y}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}w^{\mathrm{y}}_{a}\Big)\;.\end{split}

(160)

We can rearrange this as

\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}=\mu_{\mathrm{x}}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}+\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}\mathcal{\bar{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}w^{\mathrm{y}}_{a}\;,

(161)

and, wrapping up, we get

\begin{split}\hat{\varepsilon}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}^{n.d.}&=-\Lambda+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm n}^{2}w_{\rm n}^{2}+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm s}\bar{n}_{\rm s}^{2}w_{\rm s}^{2}+2\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}\bar{n}_{\rm s}\bar{n}_{\rm n}w_{\rm n}^{a}w^{\rm s}_{a}\\
&=-\Lambda+\bar{\mu}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm n}w_{\rm n}^{2}+\bar{\mu}_{\rm s}\bar{n}_{\rm s}w_{\rm s}^{2}-{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}\bar{n}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm s}w_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}\;,\end{split}

(162)

where

w_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}=g_{ab}\big(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}-w_{\mathrm{y}}^{a}\big)\big(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}-w_{\mathrm{y}}^{b}\big)\;.

(163)

It is now clear that, in order to proceed, we need an expansion for the master function,
\Lambda
.

Note that the dissipative action model assumes
\Lambda
depends on
(X_{\rm n}^{A},X_{\rm s}^{A},g_{\rm n}^{AB},g_{\rm s}^{AB},g_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{AB})
through the scalar product of the fluxes
n_{\rm n}^{2},n_{\rm s}^{2},n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}
. Therefore, we can expand
\Lambda
up to second order in the standard way (see
[
21
]
). We thus have

\begin{split}\Lambda=\Lambda_{{\rm e}}&-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{{\mathrm{x}}=n,s}{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}-{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}\delta n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}-\frac{1}{4}\sum_{{\mathrm{x}}=n,s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2})^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}}(\delta n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2})^{2}\\
&-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm n}}{\partial n_{{\rm s}}^{2}}(\delta n_{\rm n}^{2})(\delta n_{\rm s}^{2})-\frac{\partial{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{\rm n}^{2}}(\delta n_{\rm n}^{2})(\delta n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2})-\frac{\partial{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{\rm s}^{2}}(\delta n_{\rm s}^{2})(\delta n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}})^{2}\;.\end{split}

(164)

To make contact with the previous expansion on the matter space we need explicit expressions for
\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}
and all other similar terms that appear in this expression.

For the four-current we have

\begin{split}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}&=n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}-\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=(\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}})\Big[\big(1+\frac{1}{2}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}\big)u^{a}+w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\Big]-\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}u^{a}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\;,\end{split}

(165)

and we see that it—quite intuitively—changes both as the density and the four-velocity change. By means of
eq.

165
we get

\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}=-\big(2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\delta n^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\delta n^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}\big)=2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}+(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}})^{2}\;.

(166)

Similarly, we have

\begin{split}\delta n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}&=-\Big(\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\delta n_{a}^{\mathrm{y}}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}^{a}\delta n^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\delta n^{\mathrm{y}}_{a}\Big)\\
&=\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}+\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}+\frac{1}{2}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}w_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}\;.\end{split}

(167)

In order to complete the second order expansion of
\Lambda
we also need the products (for every possible combination) of
eq.

166
and
eq.

167
. These are found to be

\displaystyle\big(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}\big)^{2}

\displaystyle=4\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}})^{2}\;,

(168a)

\displaystyle(\delta n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2})^{2}

\displaystyle=\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}(\delta n_{\mathrm{y}})^{2}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}^{2}(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}})^{2}+2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\;,

(168b)

\displaystyle(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2})(\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}^{2})

\displaystyle=4\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\;,

(168c)

\displaystyle(\delta n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2})(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2})

\displaystyle=2\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}})\big(\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\big)\;.

(168d)

Plugging these expressions into
eq.

164
we find (up to second order)

\begin{split}\hat{\varepsilon}^{n.d.}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}&=\varepsilon_{\rm e}(\bar{n}_{\rm n},\bar{n}_{\rm s})+\bar{\mu}_{\rm n}\delta n_{\rm n}+\bar{\mu}_{\rm s}\delta n_{\rm s}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm n}\bar{c}_{\rm n}^{2}-\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm n}}_{uu}\big)(\delta n_{\rm n})^{2}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\big(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\rm s}\bar{c}_{\rm s}^{2}-\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm s}{\rm s}}_{uu}\big)(\delta n_{\rm s})^{2}-\big(\bar{\chi}^{{\rm s}{\rm n}}_{uu}+\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{uu}\big)(\delta n_{\rm n})(\delta n_{\rm s})+\bar{\mu}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm n}w_{\rm n}^{2}\\
&+\bar{\mu}_{\rm s}\bar{n}_{\rm s}w_{\rm s}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}\bar{n}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm s}w_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}\;,\end{split}

(169)

where we have made use of
eq.

162
and defined (see
Andersson and Comer 2021
)

\displaystyle\bar{c}_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}

\displaystyle=1+2\frac{\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}{\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}\;,

(170a)

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{x}}}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\Big(\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}^{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}}+4\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}\Big)u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\doteq\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{x}}}_{uu}u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\;,

(170b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{ab}&=\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{ns}\perp_{ab}-\Big(\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}+2\bar{n}_{\rm n}^{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{\rm n}^{2}}+2\bar{n}_{\rm s}^{2}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{\rm s}^{2}}+\bar{n}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}}{\partial n_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{2}}\Big)u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\\
&\doteq\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}\perp_{ab}+\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{uu}u^{\rm e}_{a}u^{\rm e}_{b}\end{split}\;,

(170c)

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{uu}

\displaystyle=-2\bar{n}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm n}}{\partial n_{\rm s}^{2}}=-2\bar{n}_{\rm n}\bar{n}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial\bar{\mathcal{B}}^{\rm s}}{\partial n_{\rm n}^{2}}\;.

(170d)

Noting that the quantity
\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}
is the variation of the rest frame density, we can relate it to a variation of
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
and “close the loop”. Since the
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
are functions on matter space of the variables
(X_{\rm n},\;X_{\rm s},\;g^{AB}_{\rm n},\;g^{AB}_{\rm s},\;g^{AB}_{{\rm n}{\rm s}})
the expression for the energy is actually a second order expansion in terms of those variables. We note also that, because of the “two-layer structure”, the
\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}
above contain second-order terms.

A priori, the expression in
eq.

169
does not provide the total out-of-equilibrium energy because we also need to account for the dissipative terms. However, we will now show that these actually do not contribute. To do this, we assume an expansion for all the viscous stress tensors of form

S_{AB}=S^{{\rm e}}_{AB}+S_{AB}^{1}+S^{2}_{AB}+\mathcal{O}(\xi^{3})\;,

(171)

without providing (for now) the explicit expressions. Recalling
\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}u^{a}_{\rm e}=0
, we can write

S_{ab}u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}=S_{AB}(X_{{\rm e}}^{A}+\delta X^{A})_{,a}(X_{{\rm e}}^{B}+\delta X^{B})_{,b}u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}=S_{AB}^{{\rm e}}\,\delta X^{A}_{\;,a}\,\delta X^{B}_{\;,b}u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}\;,

(172)

where the expansion is up to second order. It is clear that this argument is valid for each viscous stress tensor, and for
D_{ab}u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}
as well, so that the dissipative contributions to the off-equilibrium energy are, at least, of second order. Assuming that the energy is stationary, that is

\hat{\varepsilon}^{n.d.}_{{\rm o}.{\rm e}.}-\varepsilon_{\rm e}(\bar{n}_{\rm n},\bar{n}_{\rm s})=\mathcal{O}(\xi^{2})\;,

(173)

we then have

\bar{\mu}_{\rm n}\delta n_{\rm n}+\bar{\mu}_{\rm s}\delta n_{\rm s}=\mathcal{O}(\xi^{2})\;,

(174)

which has a clear thermodynamical interpretation and is consistent with the EIT picture, since, up to first order, the generalized energy is a function of the
n_{\mathrm{x}}
only.

We want to translate the above result into conditions for the matter space functions
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
. We start by observing that in the conservative case, the three-form
n_{ABC}^{\mathrm{x}}
is a function of the
X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}
coordinates only. Therefore,
\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
is just a function of
X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}
, while, because
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}=\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}\sqrt{g^{\mathrm{x}}}
, the latter depends also on the projected metric

\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{g^{\mathrm{x}}}\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}=\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}\;.

(175)

When considering the expansion of
n_{\mathrm{x}}
(and hence
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
) we assume that we can write

{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm e}+{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}\;,

(176)

where
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm e}
is the same as in the non-dissipative limit while the dissipative contribution
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
is a function also of the additional variables that encode the dissipation. Given the separation of
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
into two pieces it is natural to assume that
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
, but not its derivatives, vanishes at equilibrium.

Since the equilibrium evolves in a conservative fashion, we can write

\begin{split}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}&\equiv{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}-{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm e}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}={\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}-{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}\Big|_{{\rm e}}\\
&=\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}}\delta X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}+\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}+\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}\delta g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}+\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}}\delta g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}+\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}\delta g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}+\mathcal{O}(2)\;,\end{split}

(177)

where here, and in similar expansions below, each quantity is to be evaluated at equilibrium. With this assumption it is easy to read off from
eq.

174
the first order relation

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm n}\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n}^{\rm d}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}=0\ .

(178)

This leads to

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm n}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\rm n}^{A}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\rm n}^{A}}=0\ ,

(179)

and analogous results for variations with respect to
X_{\rm s}^{A}
,
g_{\rm n}^{AB}
,
g_{\rm s}^{AB}
and
g_{{\rm n}{\rm s}}^{AB}
follow immediately.
In particular, this shows that the total viscous stress tensor, acting on each component
D^{\mathrm{x}}_{ab}
, vanishes when the energy is stationary.

To see this explicitly we note that (see
eqs.

104
and
103
)

\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}\equiv 2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}=-2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{y}}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}=-\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{BA}\;,

(180)

where we made use of the symmetry property of the mixed metric, namely
g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}=g_{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}}}^{BA}
.
Similarly,

\begin{split}S^{{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}&\equiv 2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\Big(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}\Big)\\
&=2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\Big[\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}-\frac{1}{2}\big({\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}-{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm e}\big)g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}\Big]\\
&=2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}=-2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{y}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}=-s^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}\;.\end{split}

(181)

It is now clear that, by means of
eq.

180
and
eq.

181
, the x-species viscous stress tensor vanishes:

D^{{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}=S^{{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}+s^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}+\frac{1}{2}(\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}+\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{BA})=0\;.

(182)

We have considered the fully general case with all the additional dependences in
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}
and all the viscous tensors
S^{\mathrm{x}}_{ab}
,
\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}
and
s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}
. The same result—that is, each
D^{{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{ab}
vanishes —holds even in a less rich situation when the model is built from fewer viscous tensors. In that case we have to go back to
eq.

178
and modify the argument accordingly. It is important to stress that we have shown that the full stress-energy-momentum tensor at equilibrium is made out of just the non-dissipative part, and that the dissipative parts of the total stress-energy-momentum tensor do not contribute to the total energy density at second order.

However, we note that the energy minimum conditions in
eq.

178
do not set the purely resistive terms to zero (
eq.

102
). In fact, it only leads to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm n}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\rm n}}{\partial X_{\rm n}^{A}}=-\text{R}^{{\rm s}{\rm n},\,{\rm e}}_{A}\;,

(183a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\rm s}}{\partial X_{\rm s}^{A}}=-\text{R}^{{\rm n}{\rm s},\,{\rm e}}_{A}\;.

(183b)

The reason for this is pretty clear as these terms do not enter the expression for the energy density. We nonetheless might want to consider the case where the equilibrium equations are exactly as the conservative ones. The motivation for this can be found in the derivation of the purely resistive terms. If the different species are comoving at the action level, there is no distinction between the different
X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}
and no resistive term of this form would appear. We can enforce consistency with this observation in two ways: either we assume that we use the complete dependence on
X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}
in the conservative part, in which case

\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}}\Big|_{{\rm e}}=0\Longrightarrow\text{R}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}},\,{\rm e}}_{A}=0\;,

(184)

or, we just set the terms
{\text{R}}^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}
to zero, so that

{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm n}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\rm n}}{\partial X_{\rm n}^{A}}\Big|_{{\rm e}}={\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\rm s}}{\partial X_{\rm s}^{A}}\Big|_{{\rm e}}\;.

(185)

The latter, less restrictive assumption reminds us of the dynamical nature of chemical equilibrium in nature. Reactions happen also at equilibrium, although they do so in such a way that there is no net particle production.

Finally, it is quite easy to see that if we choose a different observer, such as the ones associated with the Eckart or Landau frame, the differences in the energy density will be of second order. Crucially, the equilibrium conditions in
eq.

178
do not depend on the choice of frame.

12
The last piece of the puzzle

In order to work out the perturbative expressions we need to expand the various dissipative terms. It should now be clear that for the viscous stress tensors we can write
49
49

49

All the derivatives are intended to be evaluated at equilibrium.

\displaystyle\delta s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{AB}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}}\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}+2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}}\Bigg)\;,

(186a)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{AB}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}+2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}\Bigg)\;,

(186b)

\displaystyle\delta S^{{\mathrm{x}}}_{AB}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}+2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}\Bigg)-{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}(\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d})g_{AB}^{\rm e}\;,

(186c)

where we recall that

\displaystyle s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{AB}=2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{y}}^{AB}}\;,

(187a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{AB}=2{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}^{\rm d}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{AB}}\;,

(187b)

\displaystyle S^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}=2\Bigg({\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}-\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{M}}^{\mathrm{x}}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}\,g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}\Bigg)\;.

(187c)

Similarly, for the “purely resistive” terms we have

\delta\text{R}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{A}=\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}+{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\Bigg)\;.

(188)

Since
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
is a function of
(X_{\mathrm{x}},\,X_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{x}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}})
, its derivatives are as well, so that we have

\begin{split}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\Bigg)=&\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{x}}^{B}\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta X^{B}_{\mathrm{x}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{B}\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta X^{B}_{\mathrm{y}}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{x}}^{BC}\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta g_{\mathrm{x}}^{BC}\\
&+\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{\mathrm{y}}^{BC}\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta g_{\mathrm{y}}^{BC}+\frac{\partial^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\partial g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{BC}\partial X_{\mathrm{y}}^{A}}\delta g_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{BC}\;.\end{split}

(189)

Similar results hold for the other variations that were not explicitly written in
eq.

186
and
eq.

188
.

Concerning the purely resistive term we note that
u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\text{R}^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}}}_{a}=0
by construction. Because we are doing an expansion with undetermined coefficients, we need to impose this by hand at every order; specifically, at the linear level. This then leads to

\delta\Big(u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\text{R}^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}}}_{a}\Big)=\text{R}^{{\mathrm{y}}{\mathrm{x}},\,{\rm e}}_{A}\Big(w_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}-\dot{\xi}^{A}_{\mathrm{x}}\Big)=0\;,

(190)

so that not only do we have
w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\dot{\xi}^{a}_{\mathrm{x}}
but also
w_{\mathrm{x}}^{A}=\dot{\xi}^{A}_{\mathrm{x}}
. This then means that we must have
50
50

50

Here the semicolon is, as usual, a short-hand notation for covariant derivative
A_{;a}=\nabla_{a}A
.

u_{\rm e}^{a}\xi_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}X^{D}_{{\rm e}\,;ba}=0
, which in turn implies that the orthogonality conditions for the viscous stress tensors

S^{\mathrm{x}}_{ab}u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}u_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}u_{\mathrm{y}}^{a}=0\;,

(191)

are automatically satisfied at linear order.

From
eq.

186
we can find the expansion for the spacetime viscous tensors through

\displaystyle\delta S^{\mathrm{x}}_{ab}=\delta S^{\mathrm{x}}_{DE}\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}-S^{\mathrm{x}}_{DE}\Big(\xi^{D}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,,a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}+\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\xi^{E}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,,b}\Big)\;,

(192a)

\displaystyle\delta s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}=\delta s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}-s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\Big(\xi^{D}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}+\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\xi^{E}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,b}\Big)\;,

(192b)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}=\delta\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}-\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\Big(\xi^{D}_{{\mathrm{x}},a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,b}+\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,a}\xi^{E}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,b}\Big)\;,

(192c)

while for the resistive terms associated with
s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}
and
\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{ab}
we have

\displaystyle\delta r^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\delta s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\nabla_{a}g_{\rm e}^{DE}-\frac{1}{2}s^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\partial_{a}\big[g^{bc}(\xi^{D}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,b}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,c}+\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,b}\xi^{E}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,c})\big]\;,

(193a)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{R}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{a}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\delta\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}\nabla_{a}g^{DE}_{\rm e}-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{S}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}_{DE}g^{bc}\Big(\xi^{D}_{{\mathrm{x}}\,,b}\nabla_{a}\Psi^{E}_{{\rm e}\,c}+\Psi^{D}_{{\rm e}\,b}\nabla_{a}\xi^{E}_{{\mathrm{y}}\,,c}\Big)\;,

(193b)

where we made use of the fact that
[\delta,\nabla_{a}]=0
because of
\delta g^{ab}=0
(see the discussion at the end of
section

10
).

Having “understood” how we may perturb the terms
R^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}
and
D^{\mathrm{x}}_{ab}
, let us focus on the remaining pieces of the equation of motion. A quick look back at
eq.

82
reveals that the only terms we still have to discuss are
\delta\Gamma_{\mathrm{x}}
and
\delta\mu^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}
. For the particle creation rate we have (see
eq.

165
)

\delta\Gamma_{\mathrm{x}}=\nabla_{a}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\dot{\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}}+\nabla_{a}(\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a})\;,

(194)

while for the x-species momentum, we get

\delta\mu^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}=\delta(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}})u^{\rm e}_{b}+\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w^{\mathrm{x}}_{b}+\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}\delta(\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}n_{\mathrm{y}})u^{\rm e}_{b}+\bar{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}w^{\mathrm{y}}_{b}\;.

(195)

Using the fact that we identified
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}
with
\mu_{\mathrm{x}}
we have

\begin{split}\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}&=\delta\big(-\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}\big)=-\big(\bar{\mu}^{\mathrm{x}}_{a}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}+\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}u^{\rm e}_{a}\big)\\
&=\delta\Big(\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}+\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}n_{\mathrm{y}}\Big)\;,\end{split}

(196)

and since
{\mathcal{B}}_{{\mathrm{x}}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}
are ultimately functions of
n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}
and
n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}
, we may use

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}=\Bigg(2n_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}+n_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}}\Bigg)\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}+\Bigg(2n_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{y}}^{2}}+n_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{B}_{\mathrm{x}}}{\partial n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}}\Bigg)\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\;,

(197a)

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}=\Bigg(2n_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}}+n_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}}\Bigg)\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}+\Bigg(2n_{\mathrm{y}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{\mathrm{y}}^{2}}+n_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{A}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}}{\partial n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2}}\Bigg)\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\;,

(197b)

in
eq.

196
. This way, making use of definitions in
eq.

170
, we arrive at

\delta{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}=\big(\bar{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}c_{\mathrm{x}}^{2}-\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{x}}}_{uu}\big)\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}-\big(\bar{\mathcal{A}}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{uu}+\bar{\chi}^{{\rm n}{\rm s}}_{uu}\big)\delta n_{\mathrm{y}}\;,

(198)

and we see that the parameters that enter the dissipative fluid equations are the entrainment coefficients (and first derivatives; that is, second order derivatives of
\Lambda(n_{\mathrm{x}}^{2},n_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}^{2})
) and the (up to second order) derivatives of the function
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}(X_{\mathrm{x}},\,X_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{x}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}})
.

Having outlined the perturbative framework, it is natural to ask how many dissipative channels the (general) model contains. Or, to be more specific, how many “dissipation coefficients” would have to be determined from microphysics? According to the expansion scheme we have developed so far, the perturbative expressions for the dissipative terms will ultimately involve all second and first order derivatives of the
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
when considered as functions of
X_{\mathrm{x}},\,X_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{x}},\,g^{AB}_{\mathrm{y}},\,g^{AB}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}
. Also, to make use of the model we need to specify the entrainment coefficients and their derivatives in the combinations from
eq.

170
. This means that the most general model one can think of contains a large number of coefficients. However, they should be, in general, known once a specific model is chosen; that is, once the explicit functional forms of
\Lambda
and the
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
have been provided. For example, if nuclear physics calculations are used to determine these explicit forms, they must be done in such a way that the constraints which arise from requiring a meaningful equilibrium configuration are taken into account, and they must ensure that the second law of thermodynamics is obeyed. If Onsager-type reasoning is invoked to ensure
\Gamma_{\rm s}
is positive (up to second order), then explicit use of

T\Gamma_{\rm s}=-D_{ba}^{\rm s}\nabla^{b}u_{\rm s}^{a}-u_{\rm s}^{a}\text{R}^{\rm s}_{a}\;,

(199)

where
T=-u^{a}_{\rm s}\mu^{\rm s}_{a}
is the temperature, would have to be made.

13
Model comparison

As an intuitive application of the formalism we have developed, it is useful to make contact with existing models for general relativistic dissipative fluids, in particular, the classic work of Landau-Lifschitz and Eckart and the second-order Müller-Israel-Stewart model. Specifically, we want to understand how standard quantities (like shear and bulk viscosity) enter the present formalism. Therefore, we need to see if the dissipative terms of the existing models can be matched with terms in the action-based description. This procedure is fairly straightforward.

The action-based model provides the total fluid stress-energy-momentum tensor, so we only have to decompose it in the usual way (cf.
eq.

27
):

T^{ab}=(\bar{p}+\chi)\perp^{ab}+\varepsilon u^{a}u^{b}+2q^{(a}u^{b)}+\chi^{ab}\;,

(200)

In this expression, the fluxes are defined with respect to some observer with four-velocity
u^{a}
. In order to be consistent with the perturbative expansion outlined above, we take this observer to be associated with the thermodynamical equilibrium, i.e.
u^{a}=u^{a}_{\rm e}
. Finally, we have split the isotropic pressure into an equilibrium contribution (denoted as
\bar{p}
as discussed above) and a non-equilibrium one.

13.1
Equating the flux currents

Let us first consider the heat. We can read off the heat flux from the total stress-energy-momentum tensor as

q^{a}=-\varepsilon u^{a}_{\rm e}-T^{ab}u^{\rm e}_{b}=-\perp^{a}_{b}T^{bc}u^{\rm e}_{c}\;.

(201)

First, let us note that there is no contribution at linear order coming from the dissipative part of the stress-energy-momentum tensor
D^{ab}
. In fact, making use of
eqs.

180
,
181
and
192
, it is easy to show that
D^{ab}u^{\rm e}_{a}=(\delta D^{ab})u^{\rm e}_{a}=\mathcal{O}(2)
. Let us therefore consider the non-dissipative part of
T^{ab}
. For the generalized pressure we have to first order

\Psi=\Lambda+\sum_{x}n_{\mathrm{x}}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}=-\bar{\varepsilon}_{\rm e}+\bar{\mu}\bar{n}+\bar{T}\bar{s}+\sum_{x=n,s}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}=\bar{p}+\sum_{x=n,s}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}\;,

(202)

where we have used the minimum energy condition (
eq.

174
) and the equilibrium Euler relation. Using

\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\hat{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}=\sum_{\mathrm{x}}n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}+\mathcal{O}(2)\approx\sum_{\mathrm{x}}\Big[\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}_{\rm e}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}_{\rm e}+\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}\big(\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}_{\rm e}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}\big)\Big]\;,

(203)

we then identify the heat flux as

q^{a}=\sum_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta n_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}=\bar{\mu}\bar{n}\,w_{\rm n}^{a}+\bar{T}\bar{s}\,w_{\rm s}^{a}\ .

(204)

Here, we have repeatedly used the Euler relation and the minimum energy condition
eq.

174
. We note that this quantity is consistent with the definition used in the classic models, see
[
21
]
.

Let us now move on to the other fluxes and, as before, first focus on the non-dissipative contribution. It is easy to check that

\begin{split}T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}=&\bigg(\bar{p}+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}\bigg)g^{ab}+(\bar{p}+\bar{\varepsilon}_{{\rm e}})u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}\\
&+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}\Big[\bar{\mu}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}u^{b}_{\rm e}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{a}+\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}u^{a}_{\rm e}\big(\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}}u^{b}_{\rm e}+{\mathcal{B}}_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}w_{\mathrm{x}}^{b}+\sum_{{\mathrm{y}}\neq{\mathrm{x}}}{\mathcal{A}}_{{\mathrm{x}}{\mathrm{y}}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{y}}w_{\mathrm{y}}^{b}\big)\Big]\;,\end{split}

(205)

so that, using the standard decomposition above one arrives at

(\bar{p}+\chi)\perp^{ab}+\chi^{ab}=\perp^{a}_{c}\perp^{b}_{d}T^{cd}=T^{ab}+T^{ad}u^{\rm e}_{d}u^{b}_{\rm e}+T^{cb}u^{\rm e}_{c}u^{a}_{\rm e}+\varepsilon u^{a}_{\rm e}u^{b}_{\rm e}\;.

(206)

If we now use the non-dissipative contribution
T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}
in this equation, we get

(\bar{p}+\chi)\perp^{ab}+\chi^{ab}=(\bar{p}+\sum_{\mathrm{x}}\bar{n}_{\mathrm{x}}\,\delta\mu_{\mathrm{x}})\perp^{ab}=(\bar{p}+\delta\Psi)\perp^{ab}\;.

(207)

That is, there may be a first-order correction in the pressure coming from
T^{ab}_{{\rm n}.{\rm d}.}
. Next, let us consider the contribution due to the non-dissipative part. From
eq.

192
we see that

\perp^{a}_{c}\perp^{b}_{d}D^{cd}=D^{ab}=\delta D^{ab}\;.

(208)

Putting everything together, we have identified

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}

\displaystyle=\delta\Psi+\frac{1}{3}g_{ab}\delta D^{ab}\;,

(209a)

\displaystyle\hat{\chi}^{ab}

\displaystyle=\delta D^{\langle ab\rangle}\;,

(209b)

\displaystyle\hat{q}^{a}

\displaystyle=\bar{\mu}\bar{n}w_{\rm n}^{a}+\bar{T}\bar{s}w_{\rm s}^{a}\;,

(209c)

where we reintroduced the “hat” to stress that these fluxes are measured by the equilibrium observer while the angle brackets mean that we are taking the trace-free symmetric part of the tensor.

13.2
Example: A viscous single fluid

We now consider the specific example of a two-component, single viscous fluid. The two species are matter, with non-equilibrium flux
n^{a}=nu^{a}_{\rm f}
, and entropy, with non-equilibrium flux
s^{a}=su^{a}_{\rm f}
. In this simple case, we assume that the non-equilibrium fluxes remain parallel
51
51

51

We note that a “real” two-fluid model would involve two independent fluid degrees of freedom
n^{a}_{\rm n},\,n^{a}_{\rm s}
. By forcing them to move together we are imposing quite strong constraints on the model. Basically, we are assuming that the timescale over which the entropy current relaxes to the particle flow is short enough that it may be neglected.
, meaning
w^{a}_{\rm n}=w^{a}_{\rm s}=w^{a}
and therefore

\displaystyle n_{\rm n}^{a}

\displaystyle=nu^{a}_{\rm f}=n(u^{a}_{\rm e}+w^{a})\;,

(210a)

\displaystyle n_{\rm s}^{a}

\displaystyle=su^{a}_{\rm f}=s(u^{a}_{\rm e}+w^{a})\;,

(210b)

where again
u^{a}_{\rm e}
is the equilibrium flow. In this case we do not have resistive terms because the two fluids are locked together from the beginning. Dissipation enters by assuming both currents depend on the (single) projected metric

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n}={\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n}(X^{A},\,g^{AB})\;,

(211a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}={\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}(X^{A},\,g^{AB})\;.

(211b)

In practice, this means that we will have additional terms due to
S^{\rm s}_{ab}
and
S^{\rm n}_{ab}
in the equations of motion.

Also, the creation rate
\Gamma_{\rm n}
has to vanish
52
52

52

The matter particle flux
n_{\rm n}^{a}
is conserved as it is identified with the baryon current.
; this implies

\Gamma_{\rm n}=-\frac{1}{\mu_{\rm n}}S^{\rm n}_{ab}\nabla^{a}u^{b}_{\rm f}=0\Longrightarrow S^{\rm n}_{ab}=0\;,

(212)

as, by construction, the viscous stress tensor
S^{\rm n}_{ab}
must be orthogonal to
u_{\rm f}^{a}
(see
eq.

104
and
[
20
]
for further details).
As a result, the final form of the non-linear equation of motion is

2n_{\rm n}^{a}\nabla_{[a}\mu^{\rm n}_{b]}+2n_{\rm s}^{a}\nabla_{[a}\mu^{\rm s}_{b]}+\Gamma_{\rm s}\mu_{b}^{\rm s}=-\nabla^{a}S^{\rm s}_{ab}\;.

(213)

Note that, when we linearize, the term involving
\Gamma_{\rm s}
will not appear in the equations, because
\Gamma_{\rm s}
has no linear contributions—entropy is expanded around a maximum, leaving only second-order terms.

Our next step is to use the expansion formalism developed in the previous sections to determine the explicit form of the viscous stress tensor
S^{\rm s}_{ab}
.
Let us start by considering the equilibrium (minimum energy) conditions. Clearly, we should have

S^{{\rm s},\,{\rm e}}_{AB}=2{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial g^{AB}}=0\Longrightarrow\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial g^{AB}}=0\;.

(214)

It also makes sense to assume
\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}/\partial X^{A}=0
. To see why, let us forget for the moment that the two species are locked together and consider:

\begin{split}R^{{\rm s}}_{A}&={\mathcal{M}}_{\rm n}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm n}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}_{\rm s}}-{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}_{\rm n}}=-{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}_{\rm s}}-{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}_{\rm n}}\\
&=-2{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}_{\rm s}}=-2{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{A}}=0\;,\end{split}

(215)

where we initially distinguished between the two constituents’ matter-space coordinates, and used the equilibrium condition. The condition
\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}/\partial X^{A}=0
is thus motivated by the fact that the resistive term vanishes (because the two currents are effectively locked).

As a result of these constraints we have
\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}=\mathcal{O}(2)
,
\delta\Psi=\mathcal{O}(2)
and the viscous stress tensor becomes (see
eqs.

186
and
192
)

\begin{split}\delta S^{\rm s}_{ab}&=\Bigg[2{\mathcal{M}}_{\rm s}\delta\bigg(\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial g^{AB}}\bigg)\Bigg]\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}\\
&=2\bar{T}\Bigg[\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{C}\partial g^{AB}}\delta X^{C}+2\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial g^{DE}\partial g^{AB}}\delta g^{DE}\Bigg]\Psi^{A}_{{\rm e}\,a}\Psi^{B}_{{\rm e}\,b}\;,\end{split}

(216)

Let us now rewrite the terms within the square brackets as

\displaystyle A_{CAB}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial X^{C}\partial g^{AB}}\;,

(217a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{DEAB}

\displaystyle=4\frac{\partial{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\partial g^{DE}\partial g^{AB}}\;.

(217b)

We further assume that
A_{CAB}
is zero as this involves degrees of freedom we do not need to recover Navier-Stokes equations.

Before moving on with the model specification, let us introduce a slight generalization to the original model from
[
20
]
. The idea is to take the normalizations
{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}
as functionals—instead of functions—of the additional variables. This does not constitute a major difference as the equations of motion, and particle production rate formulae, remain unchanged. Still, the step can be taken subject to the following caveat: The (functional) integration should extend (at most) to the spacetime region that is causally connected with each point. Here, we will assume that the analysis is done locally in space but not necessarily in time, i.e. on the world-tube formed by the spatial part of the region
\delta{\cal M}
in
fig.

2
.
In the present example this would mean

\begin{split}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}[g^{AB}]=&{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}[g^{AB}_{\rm e}]+\int\frac{\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\delta g^{AB}(x)}\delta g^{AB}(x)\text{d}^{4}x+\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\int\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{\delta g^{AB}(x)\delta g^{CD}(y)}\delta g^{AB}(x)\delta g^{CD}(y)\text{d}^{4}x\text{d}^{4}y\end{split}

(218)

where the first two terms vanish because (i)
{\mathcal{N}}^{d}_{\mathrm{x}}
vanishes at equilibrium, and (ii) the minimum energy condition.
The key step then is to replace the ordinary partial derivatives with functional derivatives in the various expressions we have discussed, so that the viscous stress tensor will be

S^{\rm s}_{AB}(x)=2\bar{T}\delta\Bigg(\frac{\delta{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\delta g^{AB}}\Bigg)(x)=2\bar{T}\int\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\delta g^{AB}(x)\delta g^{CD}(y)}\delta g^{CD}(y)\text{d}^{4}y\;.

(219)

We can now formally introduce a set of spatial coordinates
\bar{x}
comoving with the equilibrium observer and attached to the world-tube, and take the time coordinate to be the equilibrium worldline’s proper time
\tau
. Also, to enforce locality in space we let

\frac{\delta^{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{\delta g^{AB}(x)\delta g^{CD}(y)}=\frac{1}{4}\Sigma_{ABCD}(\bar{x},\tau_{x}-\tau_{y})\,\delta^{3}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\;.

(220)

where the causality condition
\tau_{x}-\tau_{y}\geq 0
is assumed to be encoded within
\Sigma_{ABCD}
.

Let us first of all, as a consistency check, show that the formula for the particle production rate remains unaltered by these modifications. We have (see
eq.

101
)

\begin{split}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}\Gamma_{\mathrm{x}}&=\frac{1}{3!}\mu_{\mathrm{x}}^{ABC}\frac{dn^{\mathrm{x}}_{ABC}}{d\tau_{\mathrm{x}}}=\bar{\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\frac{d}{d\tau_{\mathrm{x}}}\big(\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm e}+\bar{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}\big)\\
&={\mathcal{M}}_{\mathrm{x}}\Bigg(\frac{d{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{d\tau_{\mathrm{x}}}+\frac{1}{2}{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}g^{\mathrm{x}}_{AB}\frac{dg_{\mathrm{x}}^{AB}}{d\tau_{\mathrm{x}}}\Bigg)\;.\end{split}

(221)

where, for a single viscous fluid the result simplifies to

\Gamma_{\mathrm{x}}=\frac{d{\mathcal{N}}_{\mathrm{x}}^{\rm d}}{d\tau}+\mathcal{O}(3)\;.

(222)

If in particular we consider the entropy production rate
\Gamma_{\rm s}
we have

\begin{split}{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}&=\frac{1}{8}\int\Sigma_{ABCD}(\bar{x},\tau-\tau^{\prime})\delta g^{AB}(\bar{x},\tau)\delta g^{CD}(\bar{x},\tau^{\prime})\text{d}^{3}\bar{x}\,\text{d}\tau\,\text{d}\tau^{\prime}\;,\end{split}

(223)

To compute the entropy creation rate we have to use the chain rule (generalized to functionals) on
{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}[g^{AB}(x)]

\frac{d{\mathcal{N}}_{\rm s}^{\rm d}}{d\tau}=\int\frac{\delta N_{\rm s}^{d}}{\delta g^{AB}(y)}\frac{\delta g^{AB}(y)}{\delta\tau(x)}\text{d}^{4}y\;.

(224)

But, because
g^{AB}
is a “normal” function of the spacetime coordinates

\frac{\delta g^{AB}(y)}{\delta\tau(x)}=-2\delta^{4}(x-y)D^{(A}w^{B)}(x)\;,

(225)

so that we are left with

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\rm s}&=-\frac{1}{\bar{T}}S_{AB}(\bar{x},\tau)D^{(A}w^{B)}(\bar{x},\tau)\;.\end{split}

(226)

We can now make use of this “functional generalization” to recover the Navier-Stokes model for a bulk- and shear viscous fluid. To focus on the key point, let us consider first the purely bulk-viscous case

S(\tau)=\int K(\tau-\tau^{\prime})A(\tau^{\prime})\text{d}\tau^{\prime}\;,

(227)

where
S
represents the trace of the viscous-stress tensor while A stands for the trace
\text{tr }\delta g^{AB}
. Because of the difference between
\delta g^{AB}
and
\dot{g}^{AB}
—the former involves gradients in the displacement while the latter depends on the velocity—in order to recover a Navier-Stokes model we need to take

K(\tau-\tau^{\prime})=-T\zeta\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}\delta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\;,

(228)

since this would give

S(\tau)=T\zeta\int[-\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}\delta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})]A(\tau^{\prime})\text{d}\tau^{\prime}=T\zeta\int\delta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}A(\tau^{\prime})\text{d}\tau^{\prime}=T\zeta\frac{dA}{\text{d}\tau}\;,

(229)

as desired. We now implement this for the bulk- and shear model. We can do this using the standard decomposition of the bulk and shear response as

\Sigma_{ABCD}=\Sigma_{ABCD}^{\rm b}+\Sigma_{ABCD}^{\rm s}\;,

(230)

with

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ABCD}^{\rm b}

\displaystyle=\frac{\zeta(x)}{\bar{T}}\,g^{\rm e}_{AB}g^{\rm e}_{CD}\delta^{3}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\,q_{\rm b}(\tau_{x}-\tau_{y})\;,

(231a)

\displaystyle\Sigma_{ABCD}^{\rm s}

\displaystyle=2\frac{\eta(x)}{\bar{T}}\,\bigg(g^{\rm e}_{A(C}g^{\rm e}_{B)D}-\frac{2}{3}g^{\rm e}_{AB}g^{\rm e}_{CD}\bigg)\delta^{3}(\bar{x}-\bar{y})\,q_{\rm s}(\tau_{x}-\tau_{y})\;,

(231b)

where the two kernels would be
53
53

53

Note that we have chosen to separate the bulk- and shear channels as usual, even though the present construction allows for anisotropic response in the velocity gradients to viscosity relation. We have also introduced two independent kernels to allow for different response to bulk and shear strain rates.
the same
q_{\rm b}=q_{\rm s}=-\partial_{\tau_{y}}\delta(\tau_{x}-\tau_{y})
. It then follows that the only viscosity tensor of the model is

S^{\rm s}_{ab}=\chi_{ab}+\chi\perp_{ab}=\frac{1}{3}\zeta\,\theta\perp_{ab}+\eta\,\sigma_{ab}\;.

(232)

It is also easy to check that we can enforce compatibility with the second law by fixing the sign of the bulk-and shear viscosity coefficients. In fact,

\Gamma_{s}=\frac{\zeta}{\bar{T}}\,\theta^{2}+\frac{\eta}{\bar{T}}\,\sigma^{ab}\sigma_{ab}\geq 0\;.

(233)

where
\zeta,\eta\geq 0
. With these relations we have recovered the usual relativistic Navier-Stokes equations (the Landau-Lifschitz-Eckart model for a viscous fluid).

Let us conclude by pointing out that, to write down the full set of equations one should also expand the “Euler part” of the equation of motion, i.e. the left-hand-side of
eq.

213
. We have provided all the ingredients necessary for the explicit calculation, but leave it out here—and point to
[
21
]
for further details—as the result is not new and not key to the present discussion.

14
Cattaneo-type equations

As a practical example of the first-order expansion we outlined the model for a single (bulk and shear) viscous fluid, and showed how this leads to the expected form of the relativistic Navier-Stokes equations.
The derivation shows that the action-based formalism encodes the previous models.
It is also clear that the formalism allows us to consider much more complicated settings, should we need to do so.
However, the discussion of the first-order results is clearly not complete, because the final set of equations is widely known to suffer from causality/stability issues (see discussion in
section

5
) and it is then natural to wonder how we may fix this.
When it comes to heat-conducting systems, the way forward has been discussed in
[
142
,
22
]
, where it is demonstrated that one can resolve the stability/causality issues at first order by properly accounting for the entrainment between matter and entropy currents—retaining the compatibility with the second law.
However, for the single viscous fluid under consideration, the problem must be addressed in a different way, as the key ingredient used to solve the heat-flux case accounting for the entropy inertia, will not work for the present case as our model setting does not involve relative flows.
We now show how we can make progress in the single viscous fluid case by using a functional form that is different from
eq.

231
.
Notably, the argument stresses the importance of the “principle of memory or heredity” (see
[
121
]
).

Let us first focus on the bulk viscosity case, and then extend the results to the bulk- and shear viscous model. Recalling
eq.

227
, the first step would again be to assume

K(\tau-\tau^{\prime})=-\partial_{\tau^{\prime}}g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\;,

(234)

so that

S(\tau)=\int K(\tau-\tau^{\prime})A(\tau^{\prime})\text{d}\tau^{\prime}=\int g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\frac{dA(\tau^{\prime})}{\text{d}\tau^{\prime}}\text{d}\tau^{\prime}\;.

(235)

We can then look for the convolution kernel
g
such that the bulk-viscous scalar
S
satisfies an equation of the Cattaneo type

t_{\rm b}\dot{S}=-S-\zeta\frac{dA}{d\tau}\;,

(236)

where
t_{\rm b}
is the relaxation time-scale of the bulk-viscosity response. In terms of the convolution kernel
g
this would mean

g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})=-\frac{\zeta}{t_{\rm b}}e^{-(\tau-\tau^{\prime})/t_{\rm b}}\theta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\;,

(237)

and one can check by direct computation this leads to
eq.

236
as

t_{\rm b}\partial_{\tau}g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})+g(\tau-\tau^{\prime})=-\zeta\delta(\tau-\tau^{\prime})\;.

(238)

By inspecting the last expression, we also see that in the “fast relaxation limit”
t_{\rm b}\to 0
we recover a Navier-Stokes-type response as we would intuitively expect.
We also point to
chapter

6
where this fast relaxation limit is discussed in light of the (perhaps inevitable) resolution limitations faced in numerical implementations.

We are now ready to go back to the full bulk- and shear viscous model. We will retain the structure and symmetries from before (see
eqs.

231
and
230
), but introduce two different convolutions
q_{\rm b}
and
q_{\rm s}
to account for retarded response to bulk and shear strain rates. In essence, we have shown how we can implement a retarded response of the Cattaneo type in the action-based model by assuming that
S_{AB}
(and therefore
D_{ab}
as well) is an integral function of
g^{AB}
. The question then is, does this mean that the final fluid equations are integro-differential equations? Fortunately the answer is no. In fact, we have shown that, by a suitable choice of the response function
q(\tau-\tau^{\prime})
, the fluxes satisfy an equation of the Cattaneo-type. Therefore, instead of solving an integro-differential equation, one should treat
S^{\rm s}_{ab}=\chi\perp_{ab}+\chi_{ab}
as an unknown in
eq.

213
, and add two equations to the system

\displaystyle\chi+t_{\rm b}\,\dot{\chi}

\displaystyle=-\zeta\,\theta\;,

(239a)

\displaystyle\chi_{ab}+t_{\rm s}\,\dot{\chi}_{ab}

\displaystyle=-\eta\,\sigma_{ab}\;.

(239b)

This means that, at the end of the day, to actually solve a set of differential dissipative equations at first order, we have to treat the fluxes as additional unknowns, for which one has to provide equations that are not given by the stress-energy-momentum conservation law
\nabla_{a}T^{ab}=0
. This is reminiscent of the EIT paradigm, where one postulates from the beginning an entropy function that depends on an additional set of quantities—the thermodynamical fluxes. The difference is that the microphysical origin of the equation for the fluxes is now more clear. It is also worth noting that equations of the Cattaneo-type for the fluxes cannot be obtained in the field-theory-based models, as the constitutive equations are given in terms of the usual equilibrium variables (like
\mu,\,T
) and their derivatives—so that terms with derivatives of the fluxes (like
\dot{\chi}
) do not appear.

Equations
239
are (formally) the same as in the linearized version of Müeller-Israel-Stewart model, which has been shown to be stable and causal. In theory, nothing prevents us from choosing a different form for the retarded response
q
which could lead to acausal/unstable behaviour. However, the form of
q
suggested above has a clear physical interpretation and is microphysically motivated. If one wants to come up with an alternative, this would need to be motivated by microphysical arguments, as well.

Let us now consider the implications of the Cattaneo laws for the entropy production rate. As in the Navier-Stokes model sketched above we have

\begin{split}\Gamma_{\rm s}&=\int\Sigma_{ABCD}(x,\tau-\tau^{\prime})D^{(C}w^{D)}(\bar{x},\tau^{\prime})D^{(A}w^{B)}(\bar{x},\tau)\text{d}\tau^{\prime}=\\
&=\frac{1}{\bar{T}}S_{AB}(\bar{x},\tau)D^{(A}w^{B)}(\bar{x},\tau)\;.\end{split}

(240)

Again, let us use the bulk-viscous case to highlight the relevant features. We then have

\Gamma_{\rm s}=\frac{\zeta}{t_{\rm b}}\int_{-\infty}^{\tau}e^{-(\tau-\tau^{\prime})/t_{\rm b}}\theta(\tau)\theta(\tau^{\prime})\,\text{d}\tau^{\prime}\;,

(241)

where
\theta
is the expansion rate. It is clear that, because the expansion rate is evaluated at different times, we cannot guarantee the positivity of the entropy production rate just by fixing the sign of the bulk-viscosity coefficient as in the previous Navier-Stokes case. However, we will argue this result is not as dramatic as it may appear at first sight—at least not in the regimes relevant for physical predictions. Because of the exponential in the integral, we can assume that the values for the expansion at times
\tau^{\prime}
can be neglected for
\tau^{\prime}
that is a few
t_{\rm b}
away from
\tau
. As a result we can expand
\theta(\tau^{\prime})
as

\theta(\tau^{\prime})=\theta(\tau)+\frac{d\theta(\tau)}{\text{d}\tau}(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)=\theta(\tau)+\dot{\theta}(\tau)(\tau^{\prime}-\tau)\;,

(242)

so that we obtain

\Gamma_{\rm s}=\zeta\big[\theta^{2}(\tau)-t_{\rm b}\theta(\tau)\dot{\theta}(\tau)\big]\;.

(243)

Again, because of the product of the expansion rate with its time derivative, the entropy production rate cannot be made generally positive simply by fixing the sign of the bulk-viscosity coefficient
\zeta
. However, as discussed in
[
90
,
140
]
, physical fluid states relax—on a timescale characteristic of the microscopic particle interactions—to ones that are essentially indistinguishable from the simple relativistic Navier-Stokes description. Translated to the present context this would mean

t_{\rm b}\dot{\theta}(\tau)\approx t_{\rm b}\frac{\Delta\theta}{\tau_{\text{hydro}}}\to 0\;.

(244)

because the ratio between the relaxation timescale
t_{\rm b}
and the hydrodynamical timescale
\tau_{\text{hydro}}
is effectively negligible. As a result, for actual physical applications we can neglect the second term in the entropy production rate and compatibility with thermodynamic second law is restored. In a way, this would mean that for bulk- and shear viscous fluids, we can introduce Cattaneo laws for the fluxes—to fix the causality and stability issues of the Navier-Stokes model—while the physical content will be precisely that of Navier-Stokes.

15
Summary and outlook

We have considered the close-to-equilibrium regime of the action-based model of
Andersson and Comer 2015
for dissipative multi-fluid systems. In particular, we have shown that, starting from a set of fully non-linear dynamical equations with only the fluxes as the degrees of freedom, an expansion with respect to (a self-consistently defined) equilibrium can be introduced in a clear fashion, with the line of reasoning being similar to that of usual hydrodynamical perturbation theory.

After discussing the aspects of equilibrium which can be inferred from the action-based model itself, we established how to construct an expansion in deviations away from equilibrium in a general setting, so that the framework is of wider relevance. In the process we demonstrated the importance of the frame-of-reference of the equilibrium observer.
